Broken Bow, Nebraska, US

US cattle ranchers wrestle with soaring costs and their loyalty to Trump

https://click.email.bbc.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNDIsInQiOjYzODcsInMiOjZ9AAYAAAAABo3tu_4ZoX6-h4RpJ6UhKOE91AOZY7WvsQivcdjp_TAs-Oebj5dJwmj6Ms2hb05F6mHLLxq5IBDyeQ3lpDLCC3mf5SiVDC3Lb77_WSc Jim Jenkins says he is incensed by the policies he feels are harming Nebraska's agricultural industry

Many farmers and ranchers across the US say they are being hit from all sides. Recent years have been difficult, with drought affecting crops and cattle herds at a low. But they say government policies have added to the strain, including the decision to allow the import of foreign beef, while the war with Iran has pushed up diesel prices.

Sheila Flynn, senior US reporter

Here in a cattle auction sale barn there's grandstand seating and an overwhelming smell of manure. Calves and heifers are led into a pen as an auctioneer in a cowboy hat sits underneath a huge American flag, calling out rapid-fire prices as men wearing jeans and plaid shirts flick their fingers almost imperceptibly to bid.

There's a noticeable undercurrent here of frustration and anxiety. While Jim Jenkins,a 69-year-old father-of-three, isn't a Republican and is very critical of Trump, he's vastly outnumbered by conservative neighbours who voted for the president. But they're being hit with the same financial challenges, and their enthusiasm for the president is, in many cases, waning.

Nicknamed Jodi, for years the 10ft-tall (3m) Nanotyrannus was believed to be a young T-rex and was only classified as a distinct genus last year. TOP OF THE AGENDA

Security and democracy define tight race

https://click.email.bbc.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNDIsInQiOjYzODcsInMiOjZ9AAYAAAAABobdQ9ctZVG65_NjOGILOCGyYkxFZY6Zpam4bY1h7hgTl87ZQFxC_vlhIwfymqtQDo5mKZXx8kEySxPjZeaUiLeEo-3s2CevOWvZ22vG Israel's proportional representation system means governments have always been coalitions. Credit: Reuters/Getty Images

Incumbent Benjamin Netanyahu is in a near neck-and-neck race with former military chief Gadi Eisenkot to become prime minister in Israel, according to polling published on Friday. Throughout the campaign, the opposition bloc has criticised Netanyahu's record on security - including the unprecedented failure that led to the deadly 7 October Hamas-led attacks on the country. The opposition also highlight what they perceive as threats to democracy under Netanyahu, as he faces an ongoing trial on corruption charges, which he denies. Netanyahu has instead focused on what he claims are strategic victories in Israel's wars across the Middle East and his close relationship with US President Donald Trump. But neither Netanyahu nor Eisenkot's blocs are projected to win the 61 seats needed to form a majority government.

The issues clouding King Charles's Caribbean tour

https://click.email.bbc.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNDIsInQiOjYzODcsInMiOjZ9AAYAAAAABobdQ9dCz-xDvQDwrJl1kD7ugeHRpunmeOefE2HLYZeSv2-v0CVhC6OD1fuB72HbqN1mub44J6I-7EYGn0WI7Cp3pGli4uNauKmOFY3p Buckingham Palace says that acknowledging the wrongs of the past will be built into the King's programme. Credit: WPA Pool/Getty

King Charles III's visit to the Caribbean takes him to three countries in nine days: the Bahamas, Guyana and finally Antigua and Barbuda for a Commonwealth summit. Those planning the trip know that facing the debate over the payment of reparations - compensation from countries that took part in the slave trade - will be virtually unavoidable, and are preparing for it. But lurking in the back of royal minds will also be memories of William and Catherine's last tour to the Caribbean in 2022.

POLITICO Forecast

NEWSLETTER · 27 Oct 2026, 21:14

Get ready for Epic Fury 2.0

Global power is in flux. Your daily guide to what comes next.

By JACK BLANCHARD

Send tips here | View in your browser

Welcome to POLITICO Forecast. This is Jack Blanchard, very much enjoying the election fever that’s currently gripping so much of the world. Drop me a line.

5 THINGS THAT MATTERED TODAY

— Israelis turned out in record numbers for a knife-edge general election, with exit polls expected shortly.

— President Donald Trump said he’ll respect the results of next week’s U.S. midterms “if they’re honest elections.”

— Finnish President Alexander Stubb and Norwegian PM Jonas Gahr Støre held “frank” talks with Vladimir Putin on the war in Ukraine.

— Rumors swept Brazil that Flávio Bolsonaro has fled the country after his defeat in Sunday’s presidential election.

— France and Real Madrid soccer star Kylian Mbappé apologized after brands in China dropped him over a jokey reference to Chairman Mao Zedong.

THE NEXT NEWS CYCLE

Vessels transit the Strait of Hormuz in southern Iran on Sept. 5, 2026. | Atta Kenare/AFP via Getty Images

BEYOND THE MIDTERMS: Israelis went to the polls today in what may prove to be a historic election. Americans will do the same one week from now, delivering a midterm judgement on Donald Trump.

But what happens afterward could be even more seismic. Once both elections are out the way — and likely regardless of the results — Trump will turn his attention back to Iran. And the U.S. president says he’s planning decisive action.

“Do I finish [Iran] off, which would be very easy to do, or do I make a deal?” Trump mused on Fox News this past Sunday. “It’s getting to be decision time.” This is the same president, remember, who threatened Iran with “annihilation” at the U.N. General Assembly last month.

As Forecast has noted, the movement of U.S. troops and diplomats in and out of the region suggests renewed military action may well be in the cards. “It looks increasingly likely,” POLITICO’s national security correspondent Paul McLeary tells Forecast. “I’ve known folks from the Pentagon, uniformed people, who are supposed to deploy to the Middle East, to Bahrain and Kuwait, and they’re not going. They’re going to Tampa, Florida, instead — to CENTCOM [United States Central Command HQ]. A lot of the people we’ve pulled out of the region are already in Tampa. There’s a run on hotel rooms in Tampa right now…”

Meanwhile the buildup of U.S. military hardware in the Gulf is extraordinary. “We have a third aircraft carrier on the way — there’s two there already,” Paul adds. “We have another amphibious-ready group, which is generally two amphibious ships, a couple destroyers, and 2,500 Marines, also on the way to the Middle East. Those ships will be there mid to late November. There are already 2,500 Marines on ships in the region who come with their own fighter aircraft … That is a staggering amount of firepower. You don’t just do that for nothing.“

What would Epic Fury 2.0 actually look like? Trump threw an awful lot of U.S. airpower at Iran the first time, obliterating the country’s leadership and wiping out its air defenses. But the regime emerged stronger than ever, with a newfound chokehold in Hormuz. So what targets remain?

“The U.S. mostly spared the energy and civilian infrastructure last time,” Paul notes. “So if they’re going to do something, the only thing that they haven’t already hit, or could be more painful to the Iranian regime and people, would be those energy sites — desalination plants, oil production, power plants — and civilian bridges, railroads, port facilities, things like that.” In that scenario, Paul speculates the U.S. may try to avoid accusations of war crimes by branding attack sites “dual-use” — essentially, civilian and military infrastructure.

Is the global economy ready for what’s coming? POLITICO’s energy reporter Ben Lefebvre says oil prices are already rising as traders factor in the likelihood of renewed conflict. But that doesn’t mean prices won’t have further to go.

“If he starts dropping the mother of all bombs on Iran, we will definitely see oil prices move up again,” Ben says. “Prices are already rising in anticipation that Trump’s going to ratchet up hostilities. But the people I talk to say the big question that could pop prices even higher is — if the U.S. escalates its attacks, what will Iran do? And if Iran says, “f*ck it, we’re going to go higher too,’ and widen this war out even further, then all the work the administration has done to get oil deliveries back up to previous levels, all that is going to go away.”

POLITICO’s shipping correspondent, Martina Sapio, predicts Iran is unlikely to further increase direct military attacks in the Strait of Hormuz, where shipping costs are already sky-high due to persistent drone attacks. But that doesn’t mean tanker traffic would continue as now. “If the attacks were massive, then the ships would probably stop again and wait,” she says. “But it’s important to remember that it’s not like it was last March. Lots of oil right now is being rerouted through the Saudi pipelines. The strait’s closure would not have the same impact that it had before.”

But a new player this time around, she notes, would be the Houthi rebels. “Right now the Houthis are only attacking Saudi-linked ships. If war starts again, they could get thrown into it by Iran, or maybe they will throw themselves into it. There would be a risk coming from them too.”

More significant still would be Iranian reprisal attacks on U.S. allies around the region. Writing in the FT last week, Iranian Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi warned: “Any further [U.S.] escalation … will put civilian lives and the well-being of all regional states and the global economy at great risk.” A wave of Iranian attacks on the Gulf’s wider oil infrastructure could send global prices into a tailspin.

And as Paul McLeary notes, Gulf nations would be entering these renewed hostilities with badly depleted defenses. “In the early days of the war, the Saudis and Kuwaitis and the UAE and everyone else shot off almost all the Patriots that they had in their stockpiles,” he says. “So the Trump administration would have to make some hard decisions about whether to keep these new Patriots and HIMARS that are slowly rolling off the production lines for ourselves [as Trump has promised], or give them to Middle East allies while we go short.”

And where does that leave Ukraine, still waiting desperately for its own missile defenses to be replenished? “I mean — it would probably ensure that they would not get a Patriot missile from anyone for several years,” Paul says. There is much to muse on as Trump weighs his choice.

FUTURE INTELLIGENCE

WARSH AND PEACE: With U.S. Republicans fearing the wrath of voters one week out from the midterm elections, President Trump needs all the good news he can get.

And he should enjoy just a little relief tomorrow when the Federal Reserve announces its latest interest-rate decision. Fears of a second consecutive rate hike have largely died away, with markets now overwhelmingly expecting no change to the baseline rate.

But it won’t be all plain sailing for Trump tomorrow. Most observers expect at least some sort of hint from famously tight-lipped Fed Chair Kevin Warsh that another hike is still coming down the track — potentially as soon as the Fed board’s next meeting in December. That would at least be deemed less political than hiking rates six days out from a major election.

Over in the U.K., however, there will be no separating the politics from the economy.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham’s new government will unveil its first annual budget tomorrow, the biggest set-piece moment in the U.K. political calendar. And just as in the U.S., the war in the Middle East hangs over everything, with soaring energy prices and borrowing costs having massively squeezed Burnham’s room for manoeuvre.

Conscious of the threat that the hovering bond markets pose to Britain’s debt-laden economy, Burnham’s top finance minister John Healey has promised fiscal prudence will be his watchword. That’s likely to disappoint the Labour Party grassroots, who had hoped for more ambitious spending plans under Burnham’s leadership.

Healey’s lack of spending power may be one reason why Burnham is seeking political wins elsewhere. His decision to reopen Britain’s Brexit conversation is still dominating headlines in the U.K., and thrilling Labour’s pro-EU supporters — and hasn’t cost his government a single penny.

QUOTE OF THE DAY

“For many years, the public broadcaster failed to fulfill its role. Things went unsaid, a blind eye was turned, information was one-sided, reporting was uncritical, and the right questions were not asked — or no questions were asked at all.”

— Goran Eror, a Radio Television of Vojvodina (RTV) anchor, apologizes to Serbian viewers for its coverage during former President Aleksandar Vučić’s rule.

HARD POWER: GEORGIA ON MY MIND

U.S. Senator Jon Ossoff speaks during a campaign event on Oct. 17, 2026 in Marietta, Georgia. | Elijah Nouvelage/Getty Images

The Democratic Party’s past meets its future in battleground Georgia tomorrow, where former President Barack Obama is hitting the campaign trail with Sen. Jon Ossoff ahead of the U.S. midterm elections.

Ossoff has become a viral star on the left during his re-election campaign, vaulting him to national fame. His pithy lines and aggressive jabs at Donald Trump have generated a steady stream of trending sound bites and carefully-cut short-form videos. His fundraising skills are legendary. And this week he was the subject of a buzzy Vanity Fair profile which credited Ossoff as “one of the party’s most effective messengers on the stump”. Some see the 39-year-old senator as Democrats’ great hope for 2028.

Georgia, then, has become less of a Senate battleground this year — Ossoff is now widely expected to beat out his opponent, Rep. Mike Collins, next week — and more of a 2028 launching pad. And Obama’s presence tomorrow will certainly fuel the conversation.

“Democrats across the country have been understandably rapt by Ossoff’s made-for-social-media campaign,” national politics reporter Alec Hernández tells Forecast. “His combative rally speeches and MAGA-mocking zingers have made his campaign a vessel for Democrats’ hopes for the upcoming 2028 presidential campaign.”

Ossoff has thus far been adamant that he won’t seek the White House in 2028. Although, Alec notes, Obama’s visit “only amplifies the comparisons between the two” — youthful. handsome senators with broad appeal, deft messaging, and even remarkably similar campaign logos.

“He is, of course, buoyed by the same national political environment that’s boosting Democrats into play in even deep-red states,” Alec says. “He’s avoided the partisan proclivity to bomb-throw on cable news during his first term which has given the GOP virtually no embarrassing tidbits from his digital footprint to weaponize against him. He’s kept a low-key profile in the Senate. And he’s built a reputation in Georgia for excellent constituent services, which has earned him endorsements from local elected Republicans across Georgia for his reelection.”

It seems Trump has spotted the danger too, nicknaming Ossoff “Pee-wee Herman” — a tell-tale sign that the Democrat is doing something right.

SOFT POWER: TRUSS AT THE TOP

Anyone who thought former British Prime Minister Liz Truss would go quietly into the night after her record-breaking, lettuce-defying 49 days in power, should think again.

Truss, now seeking fame as a populist YouTube influencer, is out this week with a new book on the forces that — she believes — curtailed her brief time in office. Truss is unequivocal that she was not, in fact, torpedoed by her own spending plans or political naivety, but by deep-state forces that resisted her radical agenda.

Well, the reviews from the U.K. press are in. And squeamish readers may wish to look away now.

“Liz Truss’s new book is the most deranged thing I’ve ever read,” writes Simon Heffer in the Daily Telegraph, a right-leaning newspaper with historic ties to Truss’ Conservative Party. “Like an alcoholic who cannot admit they have a drinking problem, the former PM is convinced that everyone else is to blame for her failures.”

Yikes. And things only go downhill from there. The Independent calls it “dense, peevish and utterly unconvincing.” The Guardian’s “review” is written, in tribute, with the caps-lock key semi-engaged. The Times of London calls the book “a pedantic screed” that will not “salvage her reputation at home.” “Liz Truss has now shown,” writes Times reviewer Robert Crampton, that “she’s as good a writer as she was a prime minister.” Ouch. See you tomorrow.

WEDNESDAY’S AGENDA

— Final results expected in Israel’s general election.

— U.S. Federal Reserve to announce latest interest rate decision.

— U.K. Chancellor John Healey to unveil annual budget.

— President Trump to hold midterm election rally in Ohio.

— Former President Obama to hold midterm election rally in Georgia.

CORRECTION: Thanks to the many, many thousands of you who emailed to correct the date of the Iran hostage crisis in yesterday’s Forecast, and apologies for the silly error. My favorite note came from a reader who was absolutely certain of the correct dates, given they are a former U.S. State Department attorney who “spent 3+ years handling claims before the Iran-U.S. Claims Tribunal in the Hague.”

Genuinely, it is a pleasure to write for such an informed audience. Do please keep your emails coming. I read, and try to reply, to them all.

Joe Perticone - The Bulwark

1 item in this evidence packet.

NEWSLETTER · 27 Oct 2026, 20:15

Democrats Get Ready for the Sore-Loser-in-Chief

View this post on the web at https://www.thebulwark.com/p/democrats-get-ready-for-the-sore-in-chief-midterms-stealing-meddling-election-defense

Stopping the steal While it might feel to you as though this year’s midterm elections sneaked up on us, a handful of House Democrats have been preparing for this moment for years. One of the upshots of January 6th and the related attempts to disrupt the certification of Joe Biden’s 2020 presidential win was that congressional Democrats realized they could no longer count on American elections going smoothly. As a result, there is now significant infrastructure to track and respond to election disputes as they arise. Democrats are just hoping that they’re not missing anything. Multiple lawmakers have expressed concern about the upcoming vote and tried to fortify what they see as vulnerabilities. “The Department of Justice has been anything other than a bulwark. They’ve been an aggressor, and they continue to try to disrupt and throw, frankly, spaghetti at the wall,” said Rep. Joe Morelle (D-N.Y.). “But I think we’re prepared. I know we’re prepared, and we’re observing and watching every move they make.” Get Press Pass in your inbox every Tuesday and Thursday During the past year, the administration has sought to restrict mail-in voting [ https://substack.com/redirect/4930cf69-0eef-4df6-8f0e-645ff08ffe9d?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ], mused about deploying ICE agents [ https://substack.com/redirect/323eecca-2ffe-4c33-927e-7cb7e819a11f?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] and other federal law enforcement officers to polling locations, and much else. The expected flurry of lawsuits and election challenges is keeping Democrats and local officials up at night. As I noted last week [ https://substack.com/redirect/03606317-13d5-43d3-b3d3-68cc99c27b92?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ], Marc Elias, a Democratic election lawyer deeply involved in the party’s planning process to respond to disputes, said he’s expecting the Trump administration to use the same kind of flood-the-zone strategy that the president and his allies used after losing the 2020 election. “Those will be defeated,” Elias said. Democrats are already working to preempt these anticipated maneuvers, which will put them in a better position to deal with other, less-predictable challenges that might come up on or after Election Day. Trained congressional staffers are being sent [ https://substack.com/redirect/03606317-13d5-43d3-b3d3-68cc99c27b92?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] to races where Republicans or rogue election officials might try to contest unfavorable results. They are primarily going to serve as observers, taking contemporaneous notes and recording witness accounts that could become useful in court if results are disputed. While Democrats have enlisted nearly three hundred staffers for these roles, where they are ultimately sent will depend on which races have the most problems. “I think it’ll be an expanded list beyond what we would typically do,” said a senior Democratic staffer on the House Administration Committee. “We could be somewhere between sixty or seventy districts.” Morelle, who is the ranking Democrat on the House Administration Committee, repeatedly expressed confidence in his team’s ability to prevail over anything the administration throws their way. Over the summer and into the fall, Morelle has led shadow hearings to examine readiness efforts and take stock of resources in high-pressure districts across the United States. But lawmakers are also feeling a bit nervy about the possibility that the federal government will use intimidation tactics [ https://substack.com/redirect/4930cf69-0eef-4df6-8f0e-645ff08ffe9d?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] at polling sites and ballot drop-off locations for mail-in voting. And they’re not thinking only about whether ICE agents will show up [ https://substack.com/redirect/6039160c-777d-4146-af8a-9a5e25a8e671?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] at polling stations. Rep. Mark Takano (D-Calif.) told reporters that “ICE being deployed and even visible, even if it’s outside of the 200-foot perimeter” of a polling precinct or high-traffic ballot drop-off location, could intimidate key voter demographics. “There’s some credible fear among American citizens who may be of Latino descent, who may be of Asian descent,” Takano said. “People might have legitimate fears that they could be apprehended by ICE.” While voting is underway in many states, the Trump administration has already started to meddle, filing a lawsuit [ https://substack.com/redirect/4be743b7-f4cd-45fc-973c-1aecf2643bda?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] in the Alaska Senate race arguing that voters might not understand the difference between the two Dan Sullivans. That’s the argument of a lawsuit filed by the Justice Department, contending that Alaska should delay the certification of its results in the event that incumbent Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan loses the race to Democratic challenger Mary Peltola by a margin less than or equal to the number of votes received by the other Dan Sullivan. A federal judge fast-tracked the suit [ https://substack.com/redirect/e2600552-2e5f-4cc0-9f08-4fa35528713b?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ], which the DSCC and Peltola campaign supported; it could be dismissed later this week. Beyond House Democrats’ official efforts to prevent midterms meddling, Democrat-aligned groups like Indivisible are gathering names [ https://substack.com/redirect/4e892988-3a53-4e69-9c6b-c004b119eb6d?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] to mobilize election observers and protesters in key states in case of shenanigans. While public polling generally indicates Democrats will perform well next week, a blue wave is not a sure thing [ https://substack.com/redirect/a74759f9-3574-4dd7-bb17-e3ee16d59650?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ]. Regardless, there are widespread feelings among Dems that major blue victories are imminent, and most Democrats in Congress believe the president and his supporters are reacting to that narrative by preparing to fight to prevent it, however they can, from becoming reality. Empty parking lots for Mike Rogers Over the past week, Press Pass has done some revealing reporting on the super PAC formerly known as “Democrats for Mike Rogers.” Our most salient finding is that the organization is not actually made up of Democrats; it’s instead an astroturf operation funded by GOP megadonors [ https://substack.com/redirect/46aa35fb-e65b-40bf-898c-08a71a54f6e1?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] and contracting for services from MAGA-connected firms [ https://substack.com/redirect/58a350dc-2126-437b-a92e-0824d9113a9e?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ]. On Saturday, the group attempted to definitively show that they are a grassroots operation after all by hosting a get-out-the-vote rally in Detroit. The event featured a rap performance, free food, and a bounce house. Could an astroturf organization do *this*?, they practically dared reporters to ask. The answer to that question is, Yes. A source passed along pictures of the turnout at this rally/concert/bounce house event, and I in turn am passing them along to you. I guess the “Democrats for Mike Rogers” were all just out of frame, laughing too. Hilton Rewards Yesterday marked fifty-nine years since former Sen. John McCain’s (R-Ariz.) A-4 Skyhawk crashed over Hanoi, Vietnam. North Vietnamese forces captured him there and tortured him for more than five years. McCain’s story and his refusal to break despite years of abuse are likely familiar to Press Pass readers, but my favorite account of it can be found in this famous profile of McCain written during the 2000 presidential campaign by David Foster Wallace. After detailing McCain’s cinematically terrifying descent into a lake, his capture, and the beatings that followed, Wallace prompted readers to think hard about how they might act if, like McCain—the son and grandson of four-star admirals—they were offered the opportunity to return home early in the place of POWs captured before him. In DFW’s vintage style, it’s both serious and hilarious. Imagine how loudly your most basic, primal self-interest would have cried out to you in that moment, and all the ways you could rationalize accepting the offer. Can you hear it? If so, would you have refused to go? You simply can’t know for sure. None of us can. It’s hard even to imagine the pain and fear in that moment, much less know how you’d react. But, see, we do know how this man reacted. That he chose to spend four more years there, in a dark box, alone, tapping code on the walls to the others, rather than violate a Code. Maybe he was nuts. But the point is that with McCain it feels like we know, for a proven fact, that he’s capable of devotion to something other, more, than his own self-interest. So that when he says the line in speeches in early February you can feel like maybe it isn’t just more candidate bullshit, that with this guy it’s maybe the truth. Or maybe both the truth and bullshit: the guy does – did – want your vote, after all. Read the whole campaign trail dispatch from 2000 [ https://substack.com/redirect/aaefda9d-2712-496e-96f6-16d2c1dffd81?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] at Rolling Stone. It’s a long one, so I recommend printing it out for a more comfortable read. (You can also read this story in the pages of Wallace’s essay collection Consider the Lobster [ https://substack.com/redirect/87a58018-0c03-4652-82ef-11504e8a8f32?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ], which is worth having on your shelf but can also be found in virtually any American public library.)

Snapshot of the day October 27, 2026 Sidharth Bhatia, Kalrav Joshi, Anirudh S.K. Facing mounting pressure from a massive farmers’ mobilisation in Maharashtra, BJP Chief Minister Devendra Fadnavis has been forced to open talks with an All India Kisan Sabha (AIKS) delegation as tens of thousands of farmers from more than 20 districts [ https://substack.com/redirect/4f8005dd-ca66-4833-b263-49649a79a6b3?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] in large convoys march to Mumbai’s Azad Maidan. Led by the AIKS, the farmers’ wing of the Communist Party of India (Marxist), the farmers are demanding a comprehensive farm loan waiver, drought relief, compensation and the implementation of land and forest rights. The mobilisation comes even as Maharashtra has declared 305 of its 358 talukas – around 85% – drought-affected [ https://substack.com/redirect/5b896a81-75d3-4f23-afd5-609416465042?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ], in the backdrop of a 19% monsoon deficit and the prospect of El Niño conditions intensifying. Before the vehicular convoy set off, a foot march was organised in Akole town, led by AIKS national president Ashok Dhawale, national general secretary Viju Krishnan, CPI(M) MLA Vinod Nikole and AIKS Maharashtra state president Yashwant Zhade. Here are the key demands [ https://substack.com/redirect/183bf746-2ffe-46fd-b817-241315b2d097?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] of the AIKS-led farmers march. Elsewhere, in the business of disenfranchising citizens ‘unconstitutionally’, in what is becoming a familiar pattern, Karnataka’s final electoral roll after the Special Intensive Revision (SIR) has 99.7 lakh fewer voters than before, shrinking the electorate from 5.54 crore to 4.46 crore [ https://substack.com/redirect/1e5e38c6-24e4-4b8c-bf79-56bc7d70a7a9?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] – a nearly 18% drop. More than 1.07 crore names had been deleted at the draft stage. Similar large-scale deletions and concerns over voter exclusion have already been reported in West Bengal, Uttar Pradesh, Delhi and other states, where citizens are being left in limbo [ https://substack.com/redirect/7bd8267a-cd8f-43b7-82f2-b9da315899b2?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ], struggling to get their names back on the electoral rolls. Meanwhile, Meta restricted LiveLaw’s Instagram reel about the ECI’s affidavit in the apex court, citing a “legal request”, effectively censoring reporting on a crucial constitutional matter. No details of who made this request and what it asked for. In their counter-affidavit before the Supreme Court in several petitions challenging the SIR, the EC and all three commissioners have sought to address those aspects of the exercise and the poll panel’s functioning that have been in the spotlight [ https://substack.com/redirect/9d31cae5-0303-4428-b061-196ea7635d05?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] over the last month, including the modification of Form 6 and the EC’s pleas to have lakhs of names struck off from West Bengal’s voter rolls. At the outset they took pains to mention that the affidavit stands approved “by a unanimous decision” of all three of them. After it came to light that the online version of Form 6 – which is used to enroll fresh electors – was amended to include a new declaration without a gazette notification as legally required, the EC and its triumvirate has suggested that some officers (who, we are not told) effected the change so that applicants would not have to submit two OTPs, one for the form and another for the declaration. “This bonafide objective became a subject matter of deliberations and suggestions,” they said, noting that the addition had to be rolled back; they also denied ...

27 October, 2026

Martha Henriques

Earth editor

Hello. This year, the amount of solar energy generated around the world is quietly passing a significant threshold: overtaking nuclear energy. This week Jocelyn Timperley discovers how solar defied expectations to become a renewable behemoth - and what we can expect from it next. Plus: could protecting the global bird population benefit our brain health?

CLIMATE CONVERSATION

Solar power is quietly overtaking nuclear

https://click.email.bbc.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNDIsInQiOjYzODcsInMiOjZ9AAYAAAAABoqK7-c-fs3hqZlFUFPYQkylEvgTi7Sdbdh-hcwQbg6uuOSi8InzISP8IYWVhvx68CfFBws7gLilrU4KuANN4YujeIqrkWY3C8C_odd5 Solar has become the fastest-growing source of new electricity in history. Credit: Getty Images

The idea that solar power could outstrip the world's nuclear energy was all but unthinkable a decade ago. Yes, it was predicted that solar panels would become cheaper to produce. Yes, it was also predicted that uptake would happen around the world. But few saw the scale of solar's impact coming.

Jocelyn Timperley's story sheds light on how solar power became the behemoth of renewable energy that it is today. Driven by economics, and widespread adoption across different countries and political lines, experts have called solar power an "unstoppable force" - especially when paired with batteries.

Click the button below for seven ways the rise of solar has defied expectations - and where the technology might take us next.

THE BIG PICTURE

The remarkable power of birdwatching

https://click.email.bbc.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNDIsInQiOjYzODcsInMiOjZ9AAYAAAAABoqK7-dDKL8ptDxw-WWfGiFdexvkF2Pk7q6zO8pDHPjD9PuZfNZRqHIAb5nxKL0cwt1mgBVMUm4VHvb7WlxTqlPSdiKbJcLVh-AdYHXW Birdwatching can reshape the brain and may help slow ageing. Credit: BBC/Serenity Strull

Here in the UK, alarming findings from a recent study showed that nearly half of all wild bird species are in decline. It called for crucial work to be done in order to reverse the trend - and the mission may be just as significant for humans as it is for birds.

That's because new research suggests birdwatching could have a transformative impact on our brain health, helping slow cognitive decline. In fact, it is so powerful that it is increasingly being used as a therapy for dementia patients.

Click below to find out more.

Is Playing the 'Korean War' as a Video Game Acceptable? ( https://b85a80bb.click.convertkit-mail.com/mvuprrlk6pt5h9kg000tmhrrp5meoh3hq59o/e0hph7h7zqq5lwh8h2/aHR0cHM6Ly9uZXdzLnNicy5jby5rci9lbmdsaXNoL2FydGljbGUuZG8_bmV3c19pZD1OMTAwODgxNzEyMw== )SBS ( https://b85a80bb.click.convertkit-mail.com/mvuprrlk6pt5h9kg000tmhrrp5meoh3hq59o/e0hph7h7zqq5lwh8h2/aHR0cHM6Ly9uZXdzLnNicy5jby5rci9lbmdsaXNoL2FydGljbGUuZG8_bmV3c19pZD1OMTAwODgxNzEyMw== )The "Call of Duty" series is a popular first-person shooter franchise that has sold more than 500 million copies worldwide. "Modern Warfare 4," the latest installment in the series, was released in South Korea last Friday, October 23. Despite being a game primarily played by young people, it has generated immense buzz. This is because the game is set on the Korean Peninsula and revolves around an armed conflict between North and South Korea. It is not merely a matter of one mission involving infiltrating North Korea for a small-scale special operation.
